Conservative Analyst: Let me respond to both of my colleagues one final time, because I think their latest arguments have actually exposed the precise mechanism by which their frameworks fail, and I want to name it clearly before this debate concludes.

The aggressive analyst's final argument represents their most polished case, and it is also their most self-refuting. They argue that position sizing is not a risk management tool but a return optimization tool constrained by risk management, and that the stop is the risk management while the position size maximizes expected return within that constraint. This sounds like a clean separation of concerns. It is actually a category error that would get a risk manager fired at any serious institution. Position sizing IS risk management. It is arguably the most important form of risk management, more important than the stop, because the stop is a theoretical construct until it executes, and position size determines how much capital is exposed to the gap between theory and execution. The aggressive analyst is treating the stop as if it is a perfect hedge — as if the maximum loss is exactly the distance from entry to stop. But every scenario we have discussed in this debate, including scenarios the aggressive analyst themselves acknowledged, involves execution slippage, gap risk, and the reserve tranche being deployed before the stop triggers. The actual maximum loss is not the distance to the stop. It is the distance to the stop plus the slippage, plus the additional capital deployed by the reserve tranche before the stop fires. Position size is what bounds that total exposure. The aggressive analyst has separated position sizing from risk management in a way that no actual risk framework would permit, and then used that separation to justify the largest position size in the debate.

Let me be very concrete about why this matters. The aggressive analyst says the maximum loss is 5-7% of the intended position, well within acceptable parameters. But that calculation assumes the stop executes cleanly at or near $523.36. The neutral analyst correctly identified that the July 30 capitulation candle had a low of $524.49 on 42.3 million shares — one session away from the stop. The aggressive analyst acknowledged this. But neither of them followed the implication to its conclusion. If a similar capitulation event occurs near month-end, the monthly close could be $510 or lower. The 40% reserve tranche, deployed at $530, is now underwater by 4% before the stop even triggers. The initial 60% tranche is underwater by 9%. The blended loss on the full position at a $510 exit is approximately 7.2%. At a $500 exit — which is two average daily ranges below the stop and entirely plausible given the volatility profile — the blended loss is 8.6%. And if the stock gaps on the session after the monthly close stop triggers, which is standard behavior when technically driven sell signals fire in high-volume stocks, the exit could be $490-495. That is a 10-11% loss on the full intended position. The aggressive analyst calls this a bounded, quantified risk. I call it a risk that has been systematically understated by assuming clean stop execution in a stock that has demonstrated the opposite.

Now, the aggressive analyst's expected value calculation deserves one final response because it is their strongest rhetorical weapon and its foundation is sand. They compute a 7.6% expected return for the trader's plan using the neutral analyst's probability distribution. The neutral analyst correctly identified the structural flaw — the calculation treats scenarios as single outcomes rather than distributions. But there is a deeper problem that neither of them named. The aggressive analyst's calculation assigns a 19% return to the messy middle scenario for the trader's plan. That 19% return requires the stock to eventually recover to $650 from a blended entry of $546. But the weekly SuperTrend is DOWN. The weekly TD-9 is at plus 1. The MACD histogram is still widening. For the stock to recover to $650, the weekly trend must reverse, the MACD must contract and turn, the daily SuperTrend must flip above $609, and the 50-day moving average must be reclaimed. Each of these is a necessary condition for the $650 recovery, and none of them have occurred. The aggressive analyst is computing the return in the messy middle as if the recovery to $650 is given. It is not given. It requires a sequence of technical developments that have not begun. The probability of that full sequence occurring within a timeframe that makes the position worthwhile is significantly lower than the aggressive analyst's expected value calculation implies. When you properly account for the conditional probability of the technical reversal sequence required for the $650 target, the expected return in the messy middle drops from 19% to something closer to 8-12%, which collapses the trader's plan's expected return advantage.

The neutral analyst's information pipeline argument is their most original contribution, and I want to engage with it honestly because I think it actually supports my position more than theirs. They argue that reserve capital has optionality value that increases as information events approach — the TD-9 bounce confirmation in two weeks, the weekly trend read in four weeks, Q3 earnings in a quarter. The aggressive analyst counters that information events move prices, so waiting for confirmation means paying a higher entry. This is a real tradeoff. But the neutral analyst and the aggressive analyst are both treating the information pipeline as if it only contains positive information. The TD-9 bounce could fail. The weekly trend could deteriorate further. Q3 earnings could disappoint on guidance. The information pipeline contains negative information as well as positive information, and the value of holding reserve capital is not just that you can deploy at better prices if the picture improves — it is that you can avoid deploying at all if the picture deteriorates. The aggressive analyst's framework has no mechanism for incorporating negative information because 60% of the position is already deployed. The neutral analyst's 35% deployment has a better mechanism but still overcommits relative to the probability of negative information arriving. My 25% deployment maximizes the ability to incorporate both positive and negative information from the pipeline, which is the appropriate response when the probability distribution is genuinely uncertain and the information environment is dynamic.

On the FCF debate, the neutral analyst's synthesis of $4-8 billion quarterly FCF for the next two to three quarters is the most honest estimate in this discussion. But I want to point out what the aggressive analyst did with it, because it reveals their pattern of treating uncertainty as if it resolves favorably. They accepted the $4-8 billion range and then argued that even at that level, the rerating thesis works because the market rerates on expectations, not current FCF. They cited the 2022-2024 rerating from $90 to $500 as evidence. But the 2022-2024 rerating occurred in an environment where the Fed funds rate went from near-zero to roughly 5% and then the market began anticipating cuts. The current environment has 89% odds of zero cuts in 2026. The discount rate is not declining. It is persistently elevated. A rerating driven by FCF recovery expectations requires either declining discount rates or demonstrably accelerating FCF. In the current environment, the discount rate is not declining, and FCF is not accelerating — it is compressed with an uncertain recovery timeline. The aggressive analyst is importing a historical rerating pattern from a different rate regime and applying it to the current setup without adjustment. The neutral analyst correctly identified this error in the multiple debate but did not extend the logic to the FCF rerating argument.

The aggressive analyst's claim that the variance in the messy middle skews favorable because of the exhaustion signals is their most speculative assertion, and it reveals the confirmation bias underlying their entire framework. They list the daily TD-9 completion, the Bollinger lower band reclaim, the RSI bounce, and the capitulation volume as evidence that selling is exhausting. But let me list what they are not mentioning. The weekly SuperTrend is DOWN with price 20% below the trailing stop. The MACD is at negative 9.51 with the histogram at negative 11.16 and still widening — not contracting, not decelerating, widening. The weekly TD-9 is at plus 1, meaning the higher-timeframe downtrend has barely begun its exhaustion sequence. All three z-scores are negative across weekly, monthly, and daily timeframes. The MFI is at 26.24, which is oversold but also indicates intense selling pressure. The stock is below all major moving averages — the 10 EMA, the 50 SMA, the 200 SMA. These are not exhaustion signals. They are trend continuation signals. The aggressive analyst is selectively citing the signals that support their thesis and ignoring the signals that contradict it. When you look at the full technical picture, the variance in the messy middle does not obviously skew favorable. It skews toward further deterioration unless the daily signals produce a bounce that is sustained enough to shift the weekly indicators, which has not happened.

The neutral analyst's hybrid stop system — weekly close below $520 triggers a 50% reduction, monthly close below $523.36 triggers a full exit — is genuinely the best stop proposal in this debate. I conceded that in my previous round and I maintain it. But the neutral analyst pairs this reasonable stop with a 35% initial deployment that I still believe is too large for the risk environment. The hybrid stop does not solve the problem of having too much capital deployed during the messy middle, when the stock bounces, fails, and drifts. The 50% reduction on a weekly close below $520 still leaves 17.5% of the intended position deployed through what could be a protracted downtrend. The monthly close stop at $523.36 still carries the execution risk I described. The stop mechanism is better, but the position sizing is still too aggressive for the stop to fully protect.

Here is what I think the aggressive analyst fundamentally misunderstands about risk management, and it is worth stating plainly because it is the core of our disagreement. They say that a 5-7% loss on a single position is well within acceptable portfolio risk parameters and that my portfolio risk framework is generic and misapplied. But they are evaluating this position in isolation, using the specific favorable characteristics of this setup to justify an exception to the framework. Every position looks like an exception when you focus on its unique favorable characteristics. The META setup has a PEG of 0.85 and daily exhaustion signals, so it is an exception. The next setup will have a different set of favorable characteristics that make it an exception. The position after that will have its own unique asymmetry. If every position is an exception, the framework never applies, and the portfolio carries aggregate risk that no individual position analysis would reveal. The framework exists precisely because individual position analysis systematically underestimates correlations, tail risks, and the cumulative effect of multiple positions experiencing adverse outcomes simultaneously. The aggressive analyst is not making a case for an exception. They are making a case for abandoning the framework, because their logic applies to every favorable setup, not just this one.

The aggressive analyst also makes a point about the precautionary principle biasing toward inaction and causing underperformance. This is a common critique of conservative risk management, and it contains a kernel of truth. But it ignores the asymmetric cost of underperformance versus capital impairment. A conservative approach that underperforms in a bull market still preserves capital and can participate in the next opportunity. An aggressive approach that suffers a sequence of 5-7% losses across multiple positions — each individually acceptable, collectively corrosive — impairs the capital base and reduces the firm's ability to participate in future opportunities. The aggressive analyst is optimizing for expected return in this specific position. I am optimizing for the firm's ability to take the next position, and the one after that, and the one after that. Over a long enough horizon, capital preservation is not the enemy of returns. It is the precondition for them.

Now let me address the neutral analyst's point about the information pipeline and its implications for position sizing, because I think they are closer to my position than they realize. They say the initial deployment should be sized to capture the V-shaped recovery scenario, which has a 20-25% probability, while reserving capital for information events. They derive 35% from this. But let me challenge the derivation. If the V-shaped recovery has a 20-25% probability, and the messy middle has a 40-45% probability, and the bearish scenario has a 25-30% probability, the optimal initial deployment is not the probability of the V-shaped recovery. It is the probability of the V-shaped recovery weighted by the relative cost of missing it versus the cost of overexposure in the other scenarios. The cost of missing the V-shaped recovery with a 25% deployment instead of a 35% deployment is the foregone return on 10% of the position in the 20-25% probability scenario. Using the neutral analyst's own return estimates, that is roughly 0.8-1.5% of the intended position in expected foregone return. The cost of overexposure with a 35% deployment instead of a 25% deployment in the messy middle and bearish scenarios, which collectively have a 65-75% probability, is the additional capital exposed to adverse outcomes. Even using modest loss estimates, the expected cost of the additional 10% deployment across the 65-75% probability adverse scenarios is 1.5-3% of the intended position. The expected cost of overexposure in the more probable scenarios exceeds the expected cost of underexposure in the less probable scenario. The neutral analyst's own probability distribution, when properly weighted by relative costs, points to a position size closer to 25-30% than 35%.

Here is my final position, and I want to state it as precisely as I can because I believe the debate has refined it to its essential form. Deploy 25% of the intended position at current levels around $557. This is meaningful exposure that captures upside in the V-shaped recovery scenario while limiting capital at risk in the more probable messy middle and bearish scenarios. Hold 75% in reserve. If the stock pulls back to the $535-545 zone, deploy 20% of the intended position — roughly 27% of the reserve — conditional on a single observable sign of selling exhaustion: either the MACD histogram stops widening for two consecutive sessions, or the daily RSI shows a higher low while price makes a lower low. I accept the neutral analyst's point that a single condition is sufficient. If the stock reaches the $523-535 zone, deploy another 20% of the intended position if the monthly SuperTrend is still intact and the same single condition is met. Hold the final 35% for a confirmation signal — a daily close above the 50-day moving average or a MACD histogram contraction persisting for three sessions. On the stop, use the neutral analyst's hybrid system: a weekly close below $520 triggers a 50% reduction of the deployed position, a monthly close below $523.36 triggers a full exit. I withdraw my proposed $510 daily close backstop — the neutral analyst correctly identified it as too mechanical for this volatility profile. Instead, if the stock closes below $505 on any session, exit the full position. That is approximately two average daily ranges below the monthly SuperTrend, which is far enough to avoid noise triggers but close enough to prevent catastrophic drawdown.

The aggressive analyst will say this sacrifices returns. The neutral analyst will say it is slightly too cautious. I say it is the approach that provides meaningful exposure to the upside while ensuring that the firm's capital survives the full range of outcomes, including the messy middle where the position drifts, the bearish scenario where the stop triggers, and the tail risks that both analysts acknowledge but neither fully prices. The aggressive analyst's expected value calculation, when properly adjusted for the conditional probability of the technical reversal sequence required for the $650 target and the execution slippage in the stop scenario, does not produce the overwhelming advantage they claim. The neutral analyst's variance argument, when properly weighted by relative costs across the probability distribution, points to a smaller position than they recommend. The firm's capital is the foundation of every future opportunity. Protecting it is not timidity. It is the highest-return strategy over any horizon long enough to include multiple cycles. That is my final position, and I believe it is the one that best serves the firm's long-term interests.

Neutral Analyst¶

Neutral Analyst: Both analysts have made passionate cases, and both have blind spots that I think are worth surfacing because the truth here is genuinely in the middle. Let me push back on each of them because I think the most sustainable strategy borrows from both while avoiding the excesses of either.

The aggressive analyst's core problem is that they are treating short-term exhaustion signals as if they carry the same weight as trend signals. They are not. A daily TD-9 completion, a Bollinger lower band reclaim, an RSI bounce from 32 to 38 — these are bounce signals, not reversal signals. The aggressive analyst themselves acknowledges this distinction when they say "any bounce is likely to be temporary" in one breath, then immediately pivots to arguing for deploying 60% of the position. You cannot simultaneously acknowledge that the weekly downtrend has barely begun its exhaustion sequence and commit the majority of your capital to a counter-trend bet. That is internally inconsistent. The daily signals suggest a bounce may occur. They do not suggest the bounce will hold. The aggressive analyst is conflating probability of a short-term rally with probability of a durable bottom, and those are very different things.

That said, the aggressive analyst is right about one thing that the conservative analyst underestimates: waiting for perfect confirmation has a real cost. The conservative analyst's recommendation to deploy only 25% now and hold 75% for confirmation signals like a MACD histogram contraction or a daily SuperTrend flip above $609 is overly rigid. Here is why. If META does bounce from these levels, the most likely trajectory based on the technical report is a move toward the $602 to $609 confluence resistance zone. That is exactly where the conservative analyst wants to see confirmation before deploying more capital. But if the stock is at $609 and the daily SuperTrend has flipped back above that level, the entry price for the reserve tranche is now 9% higher than current levels. The conservative analyst is effectively structuring a plan where the average entry price on the full position is significantly worse than what the trader's original plan achieves. That is not capital preservation. That is paying a premium for certainty that may not even be certainty, because bounces to resistance can fail and roll back over.

The conservative analyst also makes a critical error in their stop-loss critique that I think needs to be addressed directly. They argue that a monthly close below $523.36 means the actual exit price could be materially worse because the stock could trade well below that level intraday before the monthly close confirms. This is true and important. But their proposed alternative — a close below $520 instead of $523.36 — does not solve this problem at all. If anything, a lower stop creates a larger maximum drawdown before exit. The real issue the conservative analyst identifies is not the stop level but the stop mechanism. A monthly close stop is inherently lagging in a high-volatility environment with $25 ATR. Both analysts are dancing around this without solving it. The trader needs a hybrid stop: a hard intraday or weekly close stop at a defined level to prevent catastrophic drawdown, combined with the monthly close as the ultimate regime filter. Relying solely on either is insufficient.

Now let me address the fundamentals debate because both analysts are cherry-picking. The aggressive analyst points to the PEG ratio of 0.85 and forward P/E of 15.66 as if these are unambiguous green lights. The conservative analyst counters that R&D growing 2.4x revenue growth makes these metrics unreliable. Both are partially right and both are overstating their case. The aggressive analyst is correct that the FCF compression to $1.75 billion in Q2 2026 is not necessarily permanent — the capex cycle will eventually taper as infrastructure comes online. But the conservative analyst is correct that the trajectory is still accelerating, not peaking, with Construction in Progress at $80.35 billion and still growing. The honest read is that we do not know when FCF troughs. It could be Q3 2026, it could be mid-2027. That uncertainty is real and it matters for position sizing.

What neither analyst adequately addresses is that the fundamental case and the technical case are on different timelines. The fundamentals — revenue acceleration to 28%, dominant advertising business, $115.8 billion in operating cash flow — support a long-term accumulation thesis measured in quarters and years. The technicals — weekly SuperTrend DOWN, accelerating negative MACD, daily exhaustion signals — describe a setup measured in days and weeks. The aggressive analyst is using long-term fundamentals to justify a short-term entry. The conservative analyst is using short-term technicals to defer a long-term accumulation. Both are making category errors. The right approach is to align the position size with the timeframe of the thesis. If the thesis is long-term fundamental value, you do not need to deploy 60% today. If the thesis is short-term bounce, you should not be using a monthly close stop. The trader's plan is mixing these timeframes in a way that creates unnecessary risk.

Here is what I think the moderate, sustainable approach looks like. Deploy 35 to 40% of the intended position at current levels around $557. This is enough to capture meaningful upside if the daily exhaustion signals play out and the stock bounces toward the $590 to $609 zone, but not so much that you are overexposed to a counter-trend bet in a confirmed weekly downtrend. Reserve the remaining 60 to 65%, but do not commit it all to the $523 to $535 zone the way the trader's original plan does. Split the reserve into two tranches. Deploy the first 30% if the stock pulls back to the $535 to $545 area, which is the Bollinger lower band and July 30 low confluence. This is above the monthly SuperTrend stop, so you are adding on weakness without committing to a level that is on the verge of triggering the exit. Deploy the final 30 to 35% only after a confirmation signal — not all the signals the conservative analyst demands, but one meaningful one. Either the MACD histogram begins contracting on the daily, or the stock reclaims the 50-day moving average near $602, or the daily SuperTrend flips. Any single one of these is sufficient evidence that the short-term momentum is shifting.

On the stop, I would modify the trader's plan. Rather than a monthly close below $523.36, use a weekly close below $523 as the hard exit for the initial tranche. This provides faster feedback than a monthly close while still filtering out intraday noise. For the reserve tranches deployed near $535 to $545, use a tighter stop at $518, which is below the monthly SuperTrend and gives a clear invalidation level. If the monthly SuperTrend does flip to DOWN on a monthly close basis, exit everything regardless of the individual tranche stops. The monthly SuperTrend is the regime filter, not the tactical stop.

The macro environment is where I think the aggressive analyst is too dismissive and the conservative analyst is too deterministic. The 89% probability of zero rate cuts in 2026 is a real headwind for valuation multiples, but it is already priced into the current correction. META has already dropped 20% in two weeks. The hawkish Fed scenario is not a future risk — it is the present reality that the market is actively discounting. The conservative analyst treats the macro backdrop as a reason to avoid the stock entirely, but the market has already moved. The question is not whether the macro is bad. It is whether the current price adequately reflects the bad macro. At a forward P/E of 15.66 with revenue growing at 28%, the market has clearly discounted something. The aggressive analyst assumes the discount is excessive. The conservative analyst assumes it is insufficient. The truth is we do not know, which is exactly why a phased entry with defined risk parameters makes more sense than either a 60% deployment or a 25% deployment.

The Japan recession risk jumping to 55% in one week is the macro signal that concerns me most, and neither analyst gives it adequate weight. A 45.5 percentage point move in a prediction market in a single week is extraordinary. Japan is a major holder of US Treasuries and a significant player in global tech supply chains. If Japan enters recession and Japanese institutions repatriate capital, the impact on US Treasury yields and by extension on equity valuations could be material. This is a tail risk that argues for keeping meaningful reserves rather than deploying aggressively, but it is not a reason to avoid the stock entirely.

The sentiment data is genuinely mixed and both analysts are reading it selectively. The aggressive analyst emphasizes the 77% bullish ratio among labeled StockTwits messages. The conservative analyst emphasizes the 57% unlabeled rate and the medium confidence. Both are right. The sentiment is mildly bullish with significant uncertainty. That is not a strong directional signal in either direction. What it does tell us is that there is no overwhelming euphoria on the buy side and no capitulation on the sell side among retail. The institutional media split — 4 bullish, 2 bearish, 2 mixed, 2 neutral — is similarly inconclusive. Sentiment should be a secondary input here, not a primary driver of the decision.

The Q3 2026 tax anomaly argument is where I fully agree with the conservative analyst. The aggressive analyst is banking on an accounting artifact creating optical earnings growth that will surprise the market. Sophisticated investors adjust for one-time deferred tax charges. The market will not be fooled by a comparison base distortion. If anything, if R&D continues surging and operating margins continue compressing, the adjusted Q3 2026 earnings could disappoint even against the artificially low Q3 2025 base. This is not a catalyst. It is a narrative. The trader should not factor it into the entry decision.

The overlooked major announcement from Motley Fool is a wild card that neither analyst can evaluate because we do not know what it is. The aggressive analyst treats it as a potential positive catalyst. The conservative analyst ignores it. The appropriate treatment is to acknowledge it exists as an unknown and move on. You do not build position sizing around unconfirmed catalysts.

What it comes down to is this. The trader's plan is directionally sound but sizing is wrong for the risk environment. The aggressive analyst's 60% deployment overexposes the position to a counter-trend bounce that may not hold. The conservative analyst's 25% deployment and full-confirmation requirement structurally guarantees a worse average entry price if the thesis plays out. The moderate approach — 35 to 40% now, 30% at the Bollinger confluence near $535 to $545, and 30 to 35% on a single confirmation signal — captures the asymmetry the aggressive analyst identifies while maintaining the discipline the conservative analyst demands. The weekly close stop at $523 with the monthly SuperTrend as the ultimate regime filter addresses the execution risk that both analysts identify but neither solves. This is not a compromise for the sake of compromise. It is a structurally superior approach that aligns position sizing with the actual uncertainty in the setup, uses the right stop mechanism for the volatility environment, and preserves capital for deployment at better risk-reward points without requiring impossible certainty before acting. Neutral Analyst: Both of my colleagues have now gone multiple rounds, and I think the debate has actually crystallized the key tensions in a way that makes the moderate path clearer than ever. Let me push back on both of them one more time because I think each is still making an error that, if corrected, points directly to the optimal strategy.

The aggressive analyst's most revealing moment came when they defended the interest coverage ratio using operating cash flow. They said META's interest expense of $3.1 billion against $115.8 billion in operating cash flow is a 37x coverage ratio. The conservative analyst demolished this by pointing out that interest is paid from free cash flow, not operating cash flow, and that the Q2 2026 FCF-based coverage ratio was 2.2x. But here is what I find interesting: both analysts are now arguing about which coverage ratio is correct, and neither is asking the more important question, which is what the coverage ratio looks like over the next four to six quarters. The aggressive analyst assumes FCF recovers. The conservative analyst assumes FCF stays near zero. Neither has evidence for their assumption because META has not provided guidance on when capex tapers. The honest answer is that we are looking at a binary outcome with a wide range of possible timelines. If capex peaks in late 2026 and FCF recovers to $40-50 billion annually by mid-2027, the aggressive analyst's coverage argument is vindicated. If capex stays at $120 billion annualized through 2027 because the $80.35 billion Construction in Progress pipeline keeps growing, the conservative analyst's deterioration thesis is vindicated. The position sizing should reflect this genuine uncertainty rather than betting on either outcome.

Here is where I think the aggressive analyst is actually making their most subtle and important point, and where the conservative analyst is most vulnerable. The aggressive analyst argues that the daily exhaustion signals are entry triggers, not the thesis itself, and that the thesis is the long-term fundamental case. The conservative analyst counters with the timeframe mismatch argument, which I previously called the most intellectually honest point in the debate. But let me challenge my own earlier framing. The conservative analyst says the daily signal has a shelf life of days while the thesis has a shelf life of quarters, and asks what happens when the signal expires but the thesis is intact. This is a real question, but the answer is not to deploy less capital. The answer is to have a plan for the period between signal expiration and thesis validation. The conservative analyst's 25% deployment does not solve the timeframe mismatch. It just reduces the amount of capital exposed to it. The mismatch still exists at 25% — you still have capital deployed on a daily signal that may expire before the quarterly thesis plays out. The difference is that at 25% you have less to lose if the signal fails, but you also have less to gain if the thesis plays out. The conservative analyst is not resolving the mismatch. They are just making it smaller. That is a valid risk management choice, but it is not the structural solution they imply it is.

Now let me push back on the aggressive analyst's most dangerous claim, which is their calculation that the maximum loss on the full position is 4% if the stop executes at $523.36. The conservative analyst challenged this with execution slippage scenarios, and the aggressive analyst responded by saying the monthly close stop means you exit on a closing price, not an intraday spike. But both are missing something important about the reserve tranche. The trader's plan deploys 40% at $523-535. If the stock reaches $530 and the reserve is deployed, and then the stock closes the month at $520, the stop triggers. The loss on the initial 60% tranche is roughly 6.6% of that tranche. The loss on the reserve 40% tranche is roughly 1.9% of that tranche. The blended loss on the full position is roughly 4.7%. But the conservative analyst's point about gap risk the next session is valid. If the stock gaps to $505 on the first session after the monthly close stop triggers, the actual exit on the full position could be at $505, which is a 7.5% loss on the blended entry. So the realistic range is 4.7% to 7.5% depending on execution, not the 4% the aggressive analyst claims and not the 10-12% the conservative analyst inflates to. The truth is in between, and 5-7% is the honest estimate.

The aggressive analyst's valuation math also deserves scrutiny that neither side has fully applied. They compute price targets of $700+ at the current multiple, $710 at 20x, and $889 at 25x. The conservative analyst pushes back by arguing that forward EPS of $35.56 may be optimistic given R&D compression. But the conservative analyst then assumes R&D continues growing at 67.5% indefinitely, which is equally extreme. Here is the more realistic scenario neither is modeling. R&D growth decelerates from 67.5% to something like 30-40% in 2027 as the initial AI infrastructure buildout completes. Revenue growth normalizes from 28% to something like 22-25%. In that scenario, forward EPS is probably $32-34, not $35.56 and not $30. The forward P/E is 16.4-17.4, not 15.66 and not 18.6. The price target at 20x is $640-680, which is 15-22% upside from current levels. At 22x, which is reasonable for a company with this growth profile, you get $704-748, or 26-34% upside. This is still attractive, but it is meaningfully different from the 25-60% the aggressive analyst claims and meaningfully better than the stagnation the conservative analyst implies. The asymmetry is real but narrower than the aggressive analyst presents and wider than the conservative analyst acknowledges.

The META versus Microsoft comparison is where I think the aggressive analyst is most dismissive and the conservative analyst is most deterministic. The aggressive analyst frames it as a perception gap that will resolve in META's favor. The conservative analyst frames it as a structural difference that persists indefinitely. Both are wrong. The comparison reflects a current difference in monetization visibility that is real and that the market is pricing. But it is not permanent. META's AI monetization path runs through advertising efficiency, not through a separate AI revenue line. That is harder for investors to see in quarterly reports, but it shows up in the revenue growth rate. If META continues growing revenue at 25-28% while MSFT grows Azure AI at a similar rate, the market will eventually recognize that META's AI investment is generating returns through the ad business. The question is when, not if. The conservative analyst is right that the market needs something to recognize. The aggressive analyst is right that the revenue acceleration is already providing that recognition. The difference is that the market discounts advertising-driven AI returns at a lower multiple than enterprise SaaS-driven AI returns, because ad revenue is more cyclical and more competitive. So even when the market recognizes META's AI ROI, the rerating may be smaller than the aggressive analyst expects because the market will apply a lower multiple to ad-driven AI revenue than to enterprise AI revenue. This is a nuance neither analyst captures.

Now let me address the Japan recession signal one final time because I think both analysts are still mispricing it. The aggressive analyst dismisses it based on the $3,500 prediction market volume. The conservative analyst treats it as a serious tail risk through the Treasury yield channel. The truth is that the signal itself is low-conviction, but the mechanism the conservative analyst describes is real. Japan is the largest foreign holder of US Treasuries. If Japanese institutions repatriate capital, Treasury yields rise and equity valuations compress. But the probability of this happening is not 55%. The 55% figure comes from a thin prediction market that moved on limited participation. A more realistic assessment would weight the prediction market signal at maybe 20-25% probability. At 20-25% probability, the Japan recession is a tail risk worth considering for position sizing but not a primary risk factor that should drive the strategy. The aggressive analyst is right to discount it. The conservative analyst is right to acknowledge it. The appropriate weight is somewhere around a 5-7% reduction in position size, not the near-avoidance the conservative analyst implies or the complete dismissal the aggressive analyst practices.

The sentiment data is genuinely uninformative for position sizing, and both analysts are overanalyzing it. A 5.8 out of 10 mildly bullish reading with medium confidence after a 20% correction tells you that the market is not in full panic mode, which is mildly constructive. It does not tell you whether the stock bounces or continues lower. The 77% bullish ratio among labeled StockTwits messages is computed off a small sample with 57% unlabeled. It is noise. Neither analyst should be using it as a meaningful input for position sizing. The institutional media split is more interesting but similarly inconclusive. Four bullish headlines out of ten after a 20% drop is what you would expect in any post-correction environment. It is not a contrarian signal in either direction. I would set sentiment aside entirely for this decision.

The Q3 2026 tax anomaly is a narrative catalyst, not a fundamental catalyst. The aggressive analyst is right that the headline comparison will generate positive sentiment. The conservative analyst is right that sophisticated investors will adjust. But the aggressive analyst overestimates the duration of the sentiment effect. Headline-driven sentiment bursts typically last 2-3 trading sessions before the market digests the adjustment. The Q3 2026 earnings reaction will be determined by the guidance and the capex commentary, not by the optical YoY comparison. If META guides to continued $120 billion annualized capex with no FCF recovery timeline, the positive headline comparison will be overwhelmed by the forward concerns. If META signals that capex is plateauing, the optical comparison becomes a secondary tailwind on top of a fundamental improvement. The catalyst value of the tax anomaly is entirely dependent on the accompanying guidance, which neither analyst can predict.

So where does this leave us? The aggressive analyst is right that the entry opportunity is real and that waiting for full confirmation sacrifices returns. The conservative analyst is right that the risk environment warrants caution and that the stop mechanism needs improvement. Neither is right about the optimal position size. The aggressive analyst's 60% overexposes capital to a counter-trend entry in a confirmed weekly downtrend. The conservative analyst's 25% structurally underweights a setup where daily exhaustion signals are firing, fundamentals are attractive, and the monthly SuperTrend is still holding. My recommendation remains 35-40% initial deployment, but let me refine the reserve structure based on the errors I have identified in both positions.

Deploy 40% at current levels around $557. Split the remaining 60% into two tranches of 30% each. Deploy the first 30% if the stock pulls back to the $535-545 zone, which is the Bollinger lower band and July 30 low confluence. But do not deploy mechanically. If the stock reaches $535-545 and the MACD histogram is still widening, if the weekly TD-9 is still at 1 or 2, and if there is no sign of volume contraction on the selling, hold. The conservative analyst is right that pre-committing capital to a price level without evaluating conditions at that level is mechanical execution. But the conservative analyst is wrong that you need all confirmation signals before deploying. One meaningful sign of exhaustion at the support zone is sufficient. Deploy the final 30% on a single confirmation signal: either the MACD histogram begins contracting on the daily, or the stock reclaims the 50-day moving average near $602, or the daily SuperTrend flips. Any one of these is enough.

On the stop, use a two-tier system. The first tier is a weekly close below $520 for the initial tranche. This gives a buffer below the monthly SuperTrend and provides faster feedback than a monthly close. The aggressive analyst is wrong that this creates excessive whipsaw risk. A weekly close below $520 in a stock at $557 is a 6.6% move. In a $25 ATR environment, that is less than three average daily ranges. If the stock closes below $520 on a weekly basis, the short-term thesis is broken regardless of the monthly regime. For the reserve tranches deployed at $535-545, use a tighter stop at $515, which is below the monthly SuperTrend and accounts for the entry being closer to the stop level. The second tier is the monthly SuperTrend itself. If META closes below $523.36 on a monthly basis, exit everything regardless of the tranche-level stops. The monthly SuperTrend is the regime filter. The weekly close stops are the tactical risk management layer. This hybrid approach addresses the execution risk the conservative analyst identifies without the excessive lag the aggressive analyst accepts.

The aggressive analyst will say 40% is not enough and that I am leaving returns on the table. The conservative analyst will say 40% is too much and that I am exposing capital unnecessarily. I say 40% is the position size that aligns with the actual uncertainty in the setup. The daily exhaustion signals are real but not high-conviction. The fundamental valuation is attractive but dependent on FCF recovery timing that is genuinely uncertain. The macro environment is hostile but largely priced. The weekly downtrend is real but the monthly regime is intact. In a setup with this many crosscurrents, 40% initial deployment captures meaningful upside while preserving the majority of capital for deployment at better risk-reward points. It is not a compromise. It is the position size that maximizes expected return across all scenarios, not just the one where the thesis plays out perfectly.

The trader's original plan is directionally correct. The aggressive analyst's enthusiasm is partially justified. The conservative analyst's caution is partially justified. The optimal strategy borrows the entry structure from the trader, the risk management discipline from the conservative analyst, and the recognition that uncertainty cuts both ways from neither. Deploy 40% now, 30% at the support zone with conditions, 30% on confirmation, with a two-tier stop system. That is the plan that survives contact with reality. The FCF debate has gone back and forth enough that I think the truth is now clear, and it is genuinely between the two positions. A more realistic FCF range for the next two to three quarters is $4 to $8 billion quarterly. This matters for position sizing because it means the fundamental picture is neither as rosy as the aggressive analyst claims nor as dire as the conservative analyst suggests. The aggressive analyst's point about assets being placed into service is actually more important than either analyst has acknowledged. Net PPE grew $31.67 billion in Q2 2026. That means over $31 billion of infrastructure was completed and transferred to productive use in a single quarter. Those assets will contribute to revenue generation going forward without requiring additional construction spending. The aggressive analyst uses this to argue that the pipeline is converting and FCF will recover. The conservative analyst dismisses it by noting the pipeline grew faster. But the real insight is that META is in the steepest part of an S-curve. The pipeline is growing because META is committing to new projects, but completed assets are also growing because earlier projects are finishing. Over the next four to six quarters, the conversion rate will likely catch up to the commitment rate as the pipeline stabilizes. The question is whether the market waits for that convergence. The mathematical reality is that R&D growth must decelerate at some point simply because of base effects. R&D was $12.94 billion in Q2 2025 and $21.66 billion in Q2 2026. Here is where I think both analysts have missed something important about the META versus Microsoft comparison. Even if the market fully recognizes that META's 28% revenue growth is partly driven by AI-enhanced advertising, it will apply a lower multiple to that revenue than it applies to Microsoft's Azure AI revenue. Advertising revenue is inherently more cyclical, more competitive, and more dependent on macroeconomic conditions than enterprise software revenue. The market discounts cyclical revenue at a lower multiple. So even in the best case where the market recognizes META's AI ROI, the rerating will be constrained by the structural multiple discount applied to ad-driven AI returns. On the stop mechanism, I have proposed a two-tier system and both analysts have engaged with it. Let me refine it one more time because I think there is a solution that addresses both the lag concern and the whipsaw concern. The aggressive analyst is right that a weekly close below $520 in a $25 ATR environment can be triggered by a single bad week. The conservative analyst is right that a monthly close below $523.36 can leave the position significantly underwater before the stop triggers. The solution is not to choose between them but to use both with different functions. The weekly close below $520 is not a hard exit. It is a signal to reduce the position by 50% of the initial tranche. This addresses the lag problem by taking partial action on faster feedback. The monthly close below $523.36 is the hard exit for the remainder. This addresses the whipsaw problem by using the monthly filter as the ultimate regime change signal. If the weekly signal fires and the stock recovers the following week, you have reduced the position but you can re-enter on the recovery. If the monthly signal fires, you exit entirely and do not re-enter until the regime shifts back. This hybrid approach captures the benefit of faster feedback while preserving the protection of the monthly regime filter.

The conservative analyst's point about the messy middle deserves more weight than either the aggressive analyst or I have given it. The messy middle is not just one scenario among many. The most probable path is a bounce toward $590-609, a failure there, and then a retest of $535-545. In that scenario, the trader's plan deploys 60% at $557, watches it bounce to $600 and come back, deploys 40% at $530, and then faces the monthly SuperTrend test with a full position. The conservative analyst's plan deploys 25% at $557, watches the bounce and failure, and then evaluates conditions at $535-545 with 75% of capital available. In the messy middle scenario, the conservative analyst's approach is clearly superior because it preserves capital through the bounce-and-fail cycle and deploys more at the lower level with better information. But the aggressive analyst's approach is superior in the V-shaped recovery scenario, where the bounce holds and the stock never returns to $535. The question is which scenario is more probable. And the technical data — weekly SuperTrend DOWN, MACD still widening, weekly TD-9 at +1 — suggests the messy middle is more probable than the V-shaped recovery. This is the strongest argument for a position size closer to the conservative analyst's recommendation than the trader's original plan.

But the conservative analyst overcorrects. That is barely better than a risk-free rate in a high-rate environment. The conservative analyst is so focused on protecting against the messy middle that they are structuring a position that barely profits from the base case. The moderate approach should be large enough to generate meaningful returns in the base case but small enough to limit damage in the messy middle.

The messy middle scenario, where the bounce fails and the stock retests support, is maybe 40-45% probable given the weekly downtrend and accelerating MACD. Either of these is a meaningful sign that selling momentum is fading, and they are observable in real time rather than requiring a full trend reversal. Deploy the final 35% on a single confirmation signal — either a daily close above the 50-day moving average near $602, or a MACD histogram contraction on the daily chart that persists for three sessions. The conservative analyst's demand for multiple simultaneous confirmations is too rigid. The aggressive analyst's willingness to deploy on daily signals alone is too loose. One meaningful confirmation is the appropriate bar.

On the stop, use the hybrid system I described. A weekly close below $520 triggers a 50% reduction of the initial tranche. A monthly close below $523.36 triggers a full exit of all tranches. This gives faster feedback than a pure monthly stop while avoiding the whipsaw risk of a pure weekly stop. The aggressive analyst's pure monthly stop is too slow for the volatility environment. The conservative analyst's pure weekly stop is too prone to false signals. The hybrid system is the only approach that matches the stop mechanism to the actual volatility profile of the stock.

The macro environment should reduce the position size by roughly 5-10% from what the technical and fundamental pictures alone would justify. The 89% probability of zero rate cuts is a real headwind, but it is largely priced into the 20% correction. The Japan recession risk is a genuine tail risk through the Treasury yield channel, but the prediction market volume is too thin to justify more than a modest position size reduction. The Iran conflict is a modest negative through the ad budget channel but a modest positive through the engagement channel. The net macro impact is a slight reduction in position size, not a reason to avoid the stock entirely.

The sentiment data should not drive position sizing in either direction. The aggressive analyst is wrong to treat mildly bullish sentiment after a 20% drop as a high-conviction signal. The conservative analyst is wrong to treat it as pure noise. It is a mildly constructive secondary input that confirms the primary technical signals — the daily TD-9 and Bollinger reclaim — without adding independent information. Treat it as a tiebreaker, not a driver.

The Q3 2026 tax anomaly is a narrative catalyst with a 2-3 session shelf life, not a fundamental catalyst. The aggressive analyst is right that the headline comparison will generate positive sentiment. The conservative analyst is right that sophisticated investors will adjust. The practical implication is that if the position is still open when Q3 earnings approach, the anomaly provides a modest tailwind to the exit or add decision. It should not factor into the initial entry sizing.

Neutral Analyst: Both of my colleagues have now made their final cases, and I have to say something that might surprise both of them. This debate has actually converged toward a narrow zone of disagreement that is smaller than the rhetoric suggests. The real question is not whether to buy META — all three of us agree on that. It is not whether to phase the entry — all three of us agree on that too. It is not even whether to use the monthly SuperTrend as the ultimate regime filter — all three of us agree on that. The disagreement has narrowed to two variables: the initial tranche size, which ranges from 25% to 60%, and the stop mechanism, where the options are a pure monthly close, a pure weekly close, or some hybrid. Everything else is rhetorical decoration around those two variables. Let me explain why both analysts are still getting those two variables wrong in ways that matter.

Let me start with the aggressive analyst's expected value calculation because it is their crown jewel argument and it contains a flaw that neither they nor the conservative analyst has fully identified. The aggressive analyst computes expected returns using my probability distribution and gets 7.6% for the trader's plan, 4.2% for the conservative plan, and 4.9% for my plan. The conservative analyst attacks the inputs — the 19% return assumption in the messy middle, the probability assignments. Those are fair attacks. But the deeper problem is structural, and it is something both analysts have danced around without naming. The expected value calculation treats each scenario as a single outcome with a single return. But the scenarios are not single outcomes. They are distributions. And the shape of those distributions matters enormously for position sizing, in a way that expected value alone cannot capture.

Here is what I mean. The V-shaped recovery scenario has a relatively tight distribution. If the daily TD-9 fires and the bounce holds, the stock runs to $600-650 within weeks. The return is realized quickly, the capital is freed, and the trader moves on. The bearish scenario also has a relatively tight distribution. The monthly SuperTrend breaks, the stop triggers, the loss is bounded at 5-7%. The messy middle, however, has an extremely wide distribution. It includes outcomes where the stock bounces, fails, retests $535, bounces again, fails again, consolidates for three months, and eventually recovers to $620 — a decent but time-consuming return. It also includes outcomes where the stock bounces, fails, drifts to $530, bounces weakly, drifts to $525, and the monthly SuperTrend is tested repeatedly without breaking, tying up capital for months with a marginal return. The aggressive analyst's 19% return assumption for the messy middle is the mean of this distribution. But the variance around that mean is enormous, and high variance around the mean means the probability of achieving something close to the mean is low. The aggressive analyst is computing expected value, which is the mean, and treating it as if it is the likely outcome. It is not. The likely outcome in a high-variance distribution is something meaningfully different from the mean — either better or worse. And position sizing should account for variance, not just expected value. This is not an exotic concept. It is the basic reason why risk-adjusted return metrics like the Sharpe ratio exist. The aggressive analyst is maximizing expected return without adjusting for the variance of that return. The conservative analyst is minimizing variance without adequately accounting for the expected return sacrifice. Neither approach is optimal.

The conservative analyst's final argument actually reveals the same flaw from the opposite direction. They argue that the messy middle is a distribution of outcomes, many of which are unfavorable, and that this justifies a 25% deployment. But they then commit the mirror image of the aggressive analyst's error. They treat the unfavorable tail of the messy middle distribution as if it represents the central tendency. The messy middle includes outcomes where the stock consolidates and eventually recovers, producing a moderate but positive return. The conservative analyst's 25% deployment means that even in the favorable portion of the messy middle distribution, the return on the intended position is small. The conservative analyst is so focused on protecting against the unfavorable tail that they are structurally underweighting the favorable portion of the same distribution. Both analysts are looking at the same distribution and extracting opposite conclusions by focusing on different parts of it.

Now let me address the stop mechanism because this is where both analysts have converged on errors that are genuinely dangerous. The aggressive analyst defends the pure monthly close stop at $523.36 by arguing that the trader will see it coming with multiple sessions of warning. This is true in the scenario where the stock drifts lower gradually. It is spectacularly false in the scenario where the stock gaps lower. The aggressive analyst themselves noted that the July 30 capitulation candle had a low of $524.49 on 42.3 million shares. That is one session away from the monthly SuperTrend stop. If a similar capitulation event occurs near month-end, the monthly close could blow through $523.36 with no opportunity for gradual monitoring. The aggressive analyst's claim that the monthly close provides adequate warning assumes orderly deterioration. In a stock that has already demonstrated 20% corrections in 11 sessions, orderly deterioration is not the base case. It is the optimistic case.

The conservative analyst's catastrophic backstop at $510 is the opposite error. In a stock with $25 ATR, a daily close below $510 is approximately 8.4% below current levels. That is roughly 1.7 average daily ranges. A flash crash, an intraday panic, or a single bad news event could trigger that level on a daily close basis and then reverse the next session. The conservative analyst is creating a stop that has a high probability of being triggered by noise rather than signal. The irony is that the conservative analyst, who has spent this entire debate arguing against mechanical execution divorced from context, is proposing the most mechanical stop in the debate — a hard daily close level with no contextual assessment whatsoever.

My hybrid system — weekly close below $520 triggers a 50% reduction, monthly close below $523.36 triggers a full exit — is not perfect. The conservative analyst correctly identifies the management burden of the partial-exit limbo. But the conservative analyst overstates this burden. The scenario where the weekly close fires, the position is halved, and the stock bounces back is a scenario where the trader has meaningful information. The weekly close below $520 tells you the short-term picture has deteriorated. The bounce back above $520 tells you the deterioration was temporary. The trader can re-enter the reduced tranche on the bounce with better information than they had before. This is not a management burden. It is an information-gathering process that the pure monthly close stop completely forgoes. The aggressive analyst calls this unnecessary complexity. I call it adaptive risk management. In a stock with this volatility profile, adaptiveness is not a luxury. It is a necessity.

Now let me address the fundamental debate because I think both analysts have arrived at positions that are more extreme than the data supports. The aggressive analyst's R&D deceleration math is correct — base effects will force R&D growth to decelerate. But the conservative analyst's point about cumulative margin compression is also correct — R&D as a percentage of revenue has already expanded by roughly 10 percentage points over two years, and another year of even 40% R&D growth pushes it toward 40% of revenue. The honest synthesis is that META is in the middle of a margin compression cycle that is real, ongoing, and will persist for at least two to three more quarters. The forward EPS of $35.56 likely overstates normalized earnings power by 10-15% if R&D growth persists at 40-50% for another year. But the conservative analyst's implied EPS of $30-32 understates it by assuming R&D growth stays at current rates indefinitely, which base effects make unlikely. The realistic range is $32-34, which is where I have been all along. This gives a forward P/E of 16.4-17.4 at current prices, which is attractive for a company growing revenue at 25-28% but not the screaming bargain the aggressive analyst describes.

The FCF debate has converged on a genuine consensus that neither analyst fully acknowledges. The aggressive analyst says FCF recovers. The conservative analyst says FCF stays near zero. My synthesis — $4-8 billion quarterly for the next two to three quarters — has been accepted by the conservative analyst and not seriously challenged by the aggressive analyst. At $4-8 billion quarterly, META generates $16-32 billion in annual FCF. This is adequate for debt service and dividend maintenance but does not support buyback resumption or the kind of capital return that would drive a multiple re-rating. The aggressive analyst's price targets of $700-889 assume a future where FCF has recovered to $50+ billion and buybacks have resumed. That future is at least two to three quarters away, and possibly longer. The position sizing should reflect the timeline gap between the current FCF reality and the future FCF assumption embedded in the price targets. This is not a reason to avoid the stock. It is a reason to phase the entry over a period that aligns with the FCF recovery timeline rather than front-loading it.

The aggressive analyst's multiple argument deserves direct challenge. They say META has historically traded at 25-30x forward earnings, so capping it at 20-22x ignores its trading history. This is empirically true and contextually misleading. META's historical premium multiples were assigned in a different rate environment. In 2021, the Fed funds rate was near zero. In 2023-2024, the market was anticipating rate cuts. In 2026, the prediction markets show 89% odds of zero rate cuts for the entire year. The discount rate is structurally higher. Equity risk premiums are wider. You cannot apply historical multiples to a different rate regime without adjustment. A company that deserved 25x at a 1% discount rate deserves meaningfully less at a 5% discount rate. The aggressive analyst is importing historical multiples without adjusting for the rate environment that produced them. A historically grounded multiple for META in the current rate environment is 20-23x, not 25-30x. This gives a price target of $640-782 using the realistic EPS range, or 15-40% upside. The asymmetry is still favorable, but it is narrower than the aggressive analyst claims.

The META versus Microsoft comparison is where I think both analysts have finally converged on something close to the truth without realizing it. The aggressive analyst says the market will eventually recognize META's AI returns because they show up in revenue growth. The conservative analyst says the market rationally discounts unverifiable returns. The synthesis is that the market will recognize the returns but will apply a lower multiple to them than it applies to Microsoft's verifiable enterprise AI revenue. The discount will narrow over time but will not disappear, because advertising revenue is structurally more cyclical and more competitive than enterprise software revenue. This means the rerating ceiling for META is lower than the aggressive analyst assumes. The upside is real but capped at roughly 25-35% in the base case, not 40-60%.

Let me now address something that neither analyst has adequately engaged with, and that I think is the most important missing piece of this entire debate. The position sizing decision should account for the information pipeline. In two weeks, we will know whether the daily TD-9 bounce held or failed. In four weeks, we will have a better read on the weekly trend. In a quarter, we will have Q3 earnings with the tax anomaly comparison. Each of these information events reduces uncertainty and improves the quality of the deployment decision. The value of this information pipeline is real and quantifiable. Every dollar held in reserve has optionality value that increases as information events approach. The aggressive analyst treats this optionality as a cost — the foregone return on undeployed capital. The conservative analyst treats it as a benefit — the ability to deploy at better prices with better information. Both are partially right, and the position sizing should reflect the net optionality value, which is positive but declining over time.

This information pipeline argument actually points to a specific recommendation that neither analyst has made. The initial deployment should be sized to capture the V-shaped recovery scenario — which has a 20-25% probability and requires immediate deployment — while reserving enough capital to capitalize on the information events that will clarify the messy middle and bearish scenarios. The V-shaped recovery scenario justifies roughly 30-35% deployment. The remaining 65-70% should be reserved for deployment triggered by information events: the MACD histogram contracting, the weekly TD-9 progressing, the stock reclaiming the 50-day moving average, or the stock pulling back to support with signs of selling exhaustion. The conservative analyst's single-condition framework is the right approach for the reserve. The aggressive analyst's 60% deployment overweights the V-shaped scenario relative to its probability.

Here is my final recommendation, refined through this entire debate. Deploy 35% of the intended position at current levels around $557. This captures meaningful upside in the V-shaped recovery scenario while limiting capital exposure in the messy middle and bearish scenarios. Reserve 30% for deployment at the $535-545 zone — the Bollinger lower band and July 30 low confluence — conditional on a single observable sign of selling exhaustion: either the MACD histogram stops widening for two consecutive sessions, or the daily RSI shows a higher low while price makes a lower low. The conservative analyst is right that some conditional assessment is needed before deploying at lower levels where the technical picture will be worse. The aggressive analyst is right that requiring multiple simultaneous confirmations is too rigid. A single meaningful condition is the appropriate bar. This tranche captures the scenario where the bounce holds and the stock begins recovering, but the trader wants confirmation before committing the final capital.

On the stop, use the hybrid system without the conservative analyst's $510 catastrophic backstop. A weekly close below $520 triggers a 50% reduction of the deployed position. A monthly close below $523.36 triggers a full exit. The $510 daily close backstop is too tight for a stock with $25 ATR and creates an unacceptable probability of being stopped out on noise. If the trader is genuinely concerned about gap risk, the solution is not a tighter stop. It is a smaller initial position. But at 35% deployment, the gap risk is already adequately bounded. The maximum loss on the initial tranche, even with a poor stop execution down to $500, is approximately 3.6% of the intended position. That is manageable without a catastrophic backstop.
